I’m just really worn out and not feeling like myself. It’s been hard to keep going like normal.\n\nDoctor: When you sleep, do you wake up feeling refreshed?\n\nPatient: No, not really. With my shifts changing around, I got to where I’d only sleep a few hours and still feel kind of wired. I know I’ve felt low a lot, but I haven’t been keeping track of whether it’s most of the day every day.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you been feeling sad or depressed?\n\nPatient: I guess… yeah, I’ve been feeling pretty sad lately. Then I’d go home and still feel like doing more, like shopping online or rearranging my apartment.\n\nDoctor: During that energized stretch, did you feel unusually confident or like you could do things other people couldn’t?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I felt like I could do things other people couldn’t. At work especially, when I’m ringing people up, every little thing pulls me off track — the scanner beeping, construction noise from the remodel, somebody walking past. I’ll just lose track of what I was doing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts that life isn’t worth living?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I’ve had thoughts like maybe everyone would be better off if I just didn’t wake up. And a few times, walking home, I caught myself thinking about just stepping off the curb into traffic. It scares me.\n\nDoctor: For the past couple of weeks, have you been feeling down or depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I know I’ve felt low a lot, but I haven’t been keeping track of whether it’s most of the day every day.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you been feeling sad or depressed?\n\nPatient: I guess… yeah, I’ve been feeling pretty sad lately. Not all the time maybe, but enough that I notice it.\n\nDoctor: During the past two weeks, have you felt sad or down nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can say nearly every day. I’ve felt down a lot, but I haven’t really been keeping track day by day.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period where you felt unusually energized or high?\n\nPatient: Yeah. There was a stretch where I felt really energized, like almost unstoppable, and I was in a really good mood. But I’d also get irritated fast, like snapping over little things, even something like being asked to count a drawer again.\n\nDoctor: During that energized stretch, did you need much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I did. With my shifts changing around, I got to where I’d only sleep a few hours and still feel wired, like I didn’t need more. And now even when I’m exhausted, I wake up early and my mind is already going.\n\nDoctor: During that energized stretch, were you more active than usual or taking on more projects?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. At work I started volunteering for extra cash-office stuff, and I’d be reorganizing shelves even when nobody asked me to. I had this whole idea for a promotion binder I was putting together. Then I’d go home and still feel like doing more, like shopping online or rearranging my apartment.\n\nDoctor: During that energized stretch, did you feel unusually confident or like you could do things other people couldn’t?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I felt like I could do things other people couldn’t. I felt more sure of myself, maybe, but not like I had some special ability or anything. Just more confident than usual, I guess.\n\nDoctor: About how long did that energized stretch last?\n\nPatient: It’s been about a month, I think. It started around when the store changed our hours for the remodel, and it’s kind of just kept going since then.\n\nDoctor: During that energized stretch, did it cause serious problems in your life or at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it did. At work I started making mistakes on the register and falling behind on the cash-office stuff, and my manager actually took me off some responsibilities I was hoping would help me get promoted. That was embarrassing.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve been avoiding my family more. It got bad enough that I needed same-day crisis help because I didn’t feel safe handling it by myself.\n\nDoctor: During that energized stretch, were you talking more than usual or so fast that other people noticed?\n\nPatient: Yeah. People at work kept telling me to slow down. I’d be talking really fast about the schedule, inventory stuff, or ideas I had for being a supervisor, and I could tell they weren’t really keeping up with me.\n\nDoctor: During that energized stretch, did you feel unusually important or powerful?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say powerful. I mean, I felt more confident than usual, like I could handle more than I probably could, but I’m not sure it was like feeling unusually important.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
